People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Well… I came in because since my grandson was killed, something in me just won’t come back. My daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand—she’s always had such warm hands, even as a little girl, I remember that—and I know I love her. I know that in my head. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like there’s a wall there, and I don’t know what to do with that.

Guilty… I don’t know. I keep turning things over, but I don’t know if guilt is the right word. When something like this happens, your mind goes looking in every cupboard for a reason, doesn’t it? I was doing that yesterday and found an old tin of buttons my mother kept—she never threw anything away, not even coat buttons. I suppose I’m the same way with thoughts. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because since my grandson was killed, something in me just won’t come back. My daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand—she’s always had such warm hands, even as a little girl, I remember that—and I know I love her. I know that in my head. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like there’s a wall there, and I don’t know what to do with that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Six days… it’s been six days since he was killed, outside the community center. I keep thinking about that place, you know, they used to have little holiday things there, and I’d bring cookies sometimes… but yes, this feeling, it started then and it hasn’t let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any sadness or depressed mood since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if sadness is the right word. People keep saying “you must be so sad,” and I suppose that’s what it ought to be, but it feels harder to name than that. I sit there and think about him, and then my mind goes off to the soup I left on the stove or the sweater he wore once—blue, I think, though maybe it was green—and then I’m just… lost in it. So yes, maybe, but I can’t quite tell what I’m feeling most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to enjoy things or find pleasure in activities that you normally would?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, my daughter sits right there with me, holding my hand, and I know I love her—of course I do. She’s my girl. Her hands have always been warm, even when she was little, she’d come in from playing and grab onto me… but now I just can’t feel that warmth inside. It’s like I know what should be there, but there’s nothing I can reach.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson passed away that you have tried to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, I suppose… I keep sitting with my daughter, because that’s something that used to make me feel better, just being near her. She’ll hold my hand, and I know it’s her, I know I love her—she used to hold my hand crossing the street when she was little, funny how that turns around—but now I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. It’s like the feeling won’t come up, no matter how close she is.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or enjoyment at all since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I keep waiting for it, you know, like when my daughter sits right next to me and takes my hand. I know I love her, I know that as sure as anything—she’s always had that way of holding on, even when she was small—but since my grandson was killed, I just… I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like the feeling is supposed to be there, but it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. Since he was killed, it’s like the things I cared about are still there, but I’m not in them anymore. Even my daughter—she’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her, I know that… I remember when she was little, she used to tuck her fingers into mine so tight—but now I can’t feel that warmth inside me. It’s just… gone, or I can’t reach it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I keep turning things over, but I don’t know if guilt is the right word. When something like this happens, your mind goes looking in every cupboard for a reason, doesn’t it? I was doing that yesterday and found an old tin of buttons my mother kept—she never threw anything away, not even coat buttons. I suppose I’m the same way with thoughts. I keep them all and sort through them, but I can’t say I know what I’m guilty of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I keep them all and sort through them, but I can’t say I know what I’m guilty of.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since your grandson passed away?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 84 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I sit there sometimes and everything feels so quiet, too quiet, like after the house has emptied out after Christmas dinner and all the plates are still stacked by the sink. My grandson used to—well, he’d always steal the corner piece of cake, you know, the one with the most icing.

But thoughts of hurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sorted that out in my head. It’s all just a blur most of the time.

It’s just… gone, or I can’t reach it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I keep turning things over, but I don’t know if guilt is the right word. When something like this happens, your mind goes looking in every cupboard for a reason, doesn’t it? I was doing that yesterday and found an old tin of buttons my mother kept—she never threw anything away, not even coat buttons. I suppose I’m the same way with thoughts. I keep them all and sort through them, but I can’t say I know what I’m guilty of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I sit there sometimes and everything feels so quiet, too quiet, like after the house has emptied out after Christmas dinner and all the plates are still stacked by the sink. My grandson used to—well, he’d always steal the corner piece of cake, you know, the one with the most icing.\n\nBut thoughts of hurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sorted that out in my head. It’s all just a blur most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: My weight? The future… I don’t really feel much when people talk about it. My daughter will sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know—of course I know—I love her. She’s my girl. But it’s like there’s no warmth getting through inside me.

I sit there and think about him, and then my mind goes off to the soup I left on the stove or the sweater he wore once—blue, I think, though maybe it was green—and then I’m just… lost in it. So yes, maybe, but I can’t quite tell what I’m feeling most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to enjoy things or find pleasure in activities that you normally would?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, my daughter sits right there with me, holding my hand, and I know I love her—of course I do. She’s my girl. Her hands have always been warm, even when she was little, she’d come in from playing and grab onto me… but now I just can’t feel that warmth inside. It’s like I know what should be there, but there’s nothing I can reach.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson passed away that you have tried to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, I suppose… I keep sitting with my daughter, because that’s something that used to make me feel better, just being near her. She’ll hold my hand, and I know it’s her, I know I love her—she used to hold my hand crossing the street when she was little, funny how that turns around—but now I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. It’s like the feeling won’t come up, no matter how close she is.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or enjoyment at all since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I keep waiting for it, you know, like when my daughter sits right next to me and takes my hand. I know I love her, I know that as sure as anything—she’s always had that way of holding on, even when she was small—but since my grandson was killed, I just… I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like the feeling is supposed to be there, but it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. Since he was killed, it’s like the things I cared about are still there, but I’m not in them anymore. Even my daughter—she’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her, I know that… I remember when she was little, she used to tuck her fingers into mine so tight—but now I can’t feel that warmth inside me. It’s just… gone, or I can’t reach it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I keep turning things over, but I don’t know if guilt is the right word. When something like this happens, your mind goes looking in every cupboard for a reason, doesn’t it? I was doing that yesterday and found an old tin of buttons my mother kept—she never threw anything away, not even coat buttons. I suppose I’m the same way with thoughts. I keep them all and sort through them, but I can’t say I know what I’m guilty of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I sit there sometimes and everything feels so quiet, too quiet, like after the house has emptied out after Christmas dinner and all the plates are still stacked by the sink. My grandson used to—well, he’d always steal the corner piece of cake, you know, the one with the most icing.\n\nBut thoughts of hurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sorted that out in my head. It’s all just a blur most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: My weight? I… I haven’t really been keeping track of that. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I don’t get on it much anymore. I used to check it before church sometimes, because one dress would fit differently than another—funny the things you notice when you’re trying to look respectable.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve noticed. Maybe my clothes feel the same, maybe they don’t. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to feel any interest or excitement about things that are happening in the future?\n\nPatient: The future… I don’t really feel much when people talk about it. My daughter will sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know—of course I know—I love her. She’s my girl. But it’s like there’s no warmth getting through inside me.\n\nIt reminds me of when the radiators used to clank in the old house but the room still stayed cold. You could hear it working, but you didn’t feel it. That’s how it is. I can know something should matter, but I can’t feel the interest or excitement of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "determine the presence of marked psychomotor agitation or retardation", "question": "Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless since your grandson passed away?"}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t… I’m not sure I’ve paid attention to that. Moving slowly, restless—those sound like things someone else might notice better than I would.

I do sit down and then get up sometimes, but then I’ll remember I meant to check whether the back door was locked, or I’ll see a cup on the table and think I should wash it, and then I don’t. My mother used to say I had “busy hands,” even when I was a girl, always folding napkins crooked at church suppers.

So I don’t know if it’s different now. I can’t really say I’ve noticed it clearly.

I keep thinking about that place, you know, they used to have little holiday things there, and I’d bring cookies sometimes… but yes, this feeling, it started then and it hasn’t let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any sadness or depressed mood since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if sadness is the right word. My grandson used to—well, he’d always steal the corner piece of cake, you know, the one with the most icing.\n\nBut thoughts of hurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sorted that out in my head. It’s all just a blur most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: My weight? I… I haven’t really been keeping track of that. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I don’t get on it much anymore. I used to check it before church sometimes, because one dress would fit differently than another—funny the things you notice when you’re trying to look respectable.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve noticed. Maybe my clothes feel the same, maybe they don’t. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to feel any interest or excitement about things that are happening in the future?\n\nPatient: The future… I don’t really feel much when people talk about it. My daughter will sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know—of course I know—I love her. She’s my girl. But it’s like there’s no warmth getting through inside me.\n\nIt reminds me of when the radiators used to clank in the old house but the room still stayed cold. You could hear it working, but you didn’t feel it. That’s how it is. I can know something should matter, but I can’t feel the interest or excitement of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’ve paid attention to that. Moving slowly, restless—those sound like things someone else might notice better than I would.\n\nI do sit down and then get up sometimes, but then I’ll remember I meant to check whether the back door was locked, or I’ll see a cup on the table and think I should wash it, and then I don’t. My mother used to say I had “busy hands,” even when I was a girl, always folding napkins crooked at church suppers.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s different now. I can’t really say I’ve noticed it clearly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Since… since it happened. Six days now, I think. It feels longer, but it’s been six days since my grandson was killed outside the community center.

I keep seeing that place in my mind, even though I try not to. They used to have bingo flyers on the board there, and a little coffee pot that never worked right… but this numbness, this wall you’re asking about—it started then. It hasn’t really lifted.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because since my grandson was killed, something in me just won’t come back. My daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand—she’s always had such warm hands, even as a little girl, I remember that—and I know I love her. I know that in my head. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like there’s a wall there, and I don’t know what to do with that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Six days… it’s been six days since he was killed, outside the community center. I keep thinking about that place, you know, they used to have little holiday things there, and I’d bring cookies sometimes… but yes, this feeling, it started then and it hasn’t let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any sadness or depressed mood since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if sadness is the right word. People keep saying “you must be so sad,” and I suppose that’s what it ought to be, but it feels harder to name than that. I sit there and think about him, and then my mind goes off to the soup I left on the stove or the sweater he wore once—blue, I think, though maybe it was green—and then I’m just… lost in it. So yes, maybe, but I can’t quite tell what I’m feeling most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to enjoy things or find pleasure in activities that you normally would?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, my daughter sits right there with me, holding my hand, and I know I love her—of course I do. She’s my girl. Her hands have always been warm, even when she was little, she’d come in from playing and grab onto me… but now I just can’t feel that warmth inside. It’s like I know what should be there, but there’s nothing I can reach.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson passed away that you have tried to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, I suppose… I keep sitting with my daughter, because that’s something that used to make me feel better, just being near her. She’ll hold my hand, and I know it’s her, I know I love her—she used to hold my hand crossing the street when she was little, funny how that turns around—but now I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. It’s like the feeling won’t come up, no matter how close she is.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or enjoyment at all since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I keep waiting for it, you know, like when my daughter sits right next to me and takes my hand. I know I love her, I know that as sure as anything—she’s always had that way of holding on, even when she was small—but since my grandson was killed, I just… I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like the feeling is supposed to be there, but it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. Since he was killed, it’s like the things I cared about are still there, but I’m not in them anymore. Even my daughter—she’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her, I know that… I remember when she was little, she used to tuck her fingers into mine so tight—but now I can’t feel that warmth inside me. It’s just… gone, or I can’t reach it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I keep turning things over, but I don’t know if guilt is the right word. When something like this happens, your mind goes looking in every cupboard for a reason, doesn’t it? I was doing that yesterday and found an old tin of buttons my mother kept—she never threw anything away, not even coat buttons. I suppose I’m the same way with thoughts. I keep them all and sort through them, but I can’t say I know what I’m guilty of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I sit there sometimes and everything feels so quiet, too quiet, like after the house has emptied out after Christmas dinner and all the plates are still stacked by the sink. My grandson used to—well, he’d always steal the corner piece of cake, you know, the one with the most icing.\n\nBut thoughts of hurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sorted that out in my head. It’s all just a blur most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: My weight? I… I haven’t really been keeping track of that. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I don’t get on it much anymore. I used to check it before church sometimes, because one dress would fit differently than another—funny the things you notice when you’re trying to look respectable.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve noticed. Maybe my clothes feel the same, maybe they don’t. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to feel any interest or excitement about things that are happening in the future?\n\nPatient: The future… I don’t really feel much when people talk about it. My daughter will sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know—of course I know—I love her. She’s my girl. But it’s like there’s no warmth getting through inside me.\n\nIt reminds me of when the radiators used to clank in the old house but the room still stayed cold. You could hear it working, but you didn’t feel it. That’s how it is. I can know something should matter, but I can’t feel the interest or excitement of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’ve paid attention to that. Moving slowly, restless—those sound like things someone else might notice better than I would.\n\nI do sit down and then get up sometimes, but then I’ll remember I meant to check whether the back door was locked, or I’ll see a cup on the table and think I should wash it, and then I don’t. My mother used to say I had “busy hands,” even when I was a girl, always folding napkins crooked at church suppers.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s different now. I can’t really say I’ve noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional numbness, like there's a wall inside you?\n\nPatient: Since… since it happened. I can’t really say I’ve noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional numbness, like there's a wall inside you?\n\nPatient: Since… since it happened. Six days now, I think. It feels longer, but it’s been six days since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nI keep seeing that place in my mind, even though I try not to. They used to have bingo flyers on the board there, and a little coffee pot that never worked right… but this numbness, this wall you’re asking about—it started then. It hasn’t really lifted.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the scale. I used to weigh myself on Sunday mornings, before church, because my sister always said a person should know where they stand, which is funny because she never knew where she put her glasses.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because since my grandson was killed, something in me just won’t come back. My daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand—she’s always had such warm hands, even as a little girl, I remember that—and I know I love her. I know that in my head. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like there’s a wall there, and I don’t know what to do with that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Six days… it’s been six days since he was killed, outside the community center. I keep thinking about that place, you know, they used to have little holiday things there, and I’d bring cookies sometimes… but yes, this feeling, it started then and it hasn’t let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any sadness or depressed mood since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if sadness is the right word. People keep saying “you must be so sad,” and I suppose that’s what it ought to be, but it feels harder to name than that. I sit there and think about him, and then my mind goes off to the soup I left on the stove or the sweater he wore once—blue, I think, though maybe it was green—and then I’m just… lost in it. So yes, maybe, but I can’t quite tell what I’m feeling most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to enjoy things or find pleasure in activities that you normally would?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, my daughter sits right there with me, holding my hand, and I know I love her—of course I do. She’s my girl. Her hands have always been warm, even when she was little, she’d come in from playing and grab onto me… but now I just can’t feel that warmth inside. It’s like I know what should be there, but there’s nothing I can reach.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson passed away that you have tried to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, I suppose… I keep sitting with my daughter, because that’s something that used to make me feel better, just being near her. She’ll hold my hand, and I know it’s her, I know I love her—she used to hold my hand crossing the street when she was little, funny how that turns around—but now I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. It’s like the feeling won’t come up, no matter how close she is.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or enjoyment at all since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I keep waiting for it, you know, like when my daughter sits right next to me and takes my hand. I know I love her, I know that as sure as anything—she’s always had that way of holding on, even when she was small—but since my grandson was killed, I just… I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like the feeling is supposed to be there, but it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. Since he was killed, it’s like the things I cared about are still there, but I’m not in them anymore. Even my daughter—she’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her, I know that… I remember when she was little, she used to tuck her fingers into mine so tight—but now I can’t feel that warmth inside me. It’s just… gone, or I can’t reach it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I keep turning things over, but I don’t know if guilt is the right word. When something like this happens, your mind goes looking in every cupboard for a reason, doesn’t it? I was doing that yesterday and found an old tin of buttons my mother kept—she never threw anything away, not even coat buttons. I suppose I’m the same way with thoughts. I keep them all and sort through them, but I can’t say I know what I’m guilty of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I sit there sometimes and everything feels so quiet, too quiet, like after the house has emptied out after Christmas dinner and all the plates are still stacked by the sink. My grandson used to—well, he’d always steal the corner piece of cake, you know, the one with the most icing.\n\nBut thoughts of hurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sorted that out in my head. It’s all just a blur most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: My weight? I… I haven’t really been keeping track of that. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I don’t get on it much anymore. I used to check it before church sometimes, because one dress would fit differently than another—funny the things you notice when you’re trying to look respectable.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve noticed. Maybe my clothes feel the same, maybe they don’t. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to feel any interest or excitement about things that are happening in the future?\n\nPatient: The future… I don’t really feel much when people talk about it. My daughter will sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know—of course I know—I love her. She’s my girl. But it’s like there’s no warmth getting through inside me.\n\nIt reminds me of when the radiators used to clank in the old house but the room still stayed cold. You could hear it working, but you didn’t feel it. That’s how it is. I can know something should matter, but I can’t feel the interest or excitement of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’ve paid attention to that. Moving slowly, restless—those sound like things someone else might notice better than I would.\n\nI do sit down and then get up sometimes, but then I’ll remember I meant to check whether the back door was locked, or I’ll see a cup on the table and think I should wash it, and then I don’t. My mother used to say I had “busy hands,” even when I was a girl, always folding napkins crooked at church suppers.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s different now. I can’t really say I’ve noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional numbness, like there's a wall inside you?\n\nPatient: Since… since it happened. Six days now, I think. It feels longer, but it’s been six days since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nI keep seeing that place in my mind, even though I try not to. They used to have bingo flyers on the board there, and a little coffee pot that never worked right… but this numbness, this wall you’re asking about—it started then. It hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the scale. I used to weigh myself on Sunday mornings, before church, because my sister always said a person should know where they stand, which is funny because she never knew where she put her glasses.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t think I’ve checked. My clothes feel like clothes, I guess. I can’t say I’ve lost a significant amount, no—not that I’ve noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yes… yes, I do. It comes in when I’m not even trying to think about him.

I’ll be folding a quilt square, or just reaching for my tea, and then all of a sudden I hear those shots again. I see him there, outside the community center, like it’s right in front of me all over again.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because since my grandson was killed, something in me just won’t come back. My daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand—she’s always had such warm hands, even as a little girl, I remember that—and I know I love her. I know that in my head. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like there’s a wall there, and I don’t know what to do with that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Six days… it’s been six days since he was killed, outside the community center. I keep thinking about that place, you know, they used to have little holiday things there, and I’d bring cookies sometimes… but yes, this feeling, it started then and it hasn’t let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any sadness or depressed mood since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if sadness is the right word. People keep saying “you must be so sad,” and I suppose that’s what it ought to be, but it feels harder to name than that. I sit there and think about him, and then my mind goes off to the soup I left on the stove or the sweater he wore once—blue, I think, though maybe it was green—and then I’m just… lost in it. So yes, maybe, but I can’t quite tell what I’m feeling most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to enjoy things or find pleasure in activities that you normally would?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, my daughter sits right there with me, holding my hand, and I know I love her—of course I do. She’s my girl. Her hands have always been warm, even when she was little, she’d come in from playing and grab onto me… but now I just can’t feel that warmth inside. It’s like I know what should be there, but there’s nothing I can reach.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson passed away that you have tried to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, I suppose… I keep sitting with my daughter, because that’s something that used to make me feel better, just being near her. She’ll hold my hand, and I know it’s her, I know I love her—she used to hold my hand crossing the street when she was little, funny how that turns around—but now I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. It’s like the feeling won’t come up, no matter how close she is.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or enjoyment at all since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I keep waiting for it, you know, like when my daughter sits right next to me and takes my hand. I know I love her, I know that as sure as anything—she’s always had that way of holding on, even when she was small—but since my grandson was killed, I just… I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like the feeling is supposed to be there, but it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. Since he was killed, it’s like the things I cared about are still there, but I’m not in them anymore. Even my daughter—she’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her, I know that… I remember when she was little, she used to tuck her fingers into mine so tight—but now I can’t feel that warmth inside me. It’s just… gone, or I can’t reach it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I keep turning things over, but I don’t know if guilt is the right word. When something like this happens, your mind goes looking in every cupboard for a reason, doesn’t it? I was doing that yesterday and found an old tin of buttons my mother kept—she never threw anything away, not even coat buttons. I suppose I’m the same way with thoughts. I keep them all and sort through them, but I can’t say I know what I’m guilty of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I sit there sometimes and everything feels so quiet, too quiet, like after the house has emptied out after Christmas dinner and all the plates are still stacked by the sink. My grandson used to—well, he’d always steal the corner piece of cake, you know, the one with the most icing.\n\nBut thoughts of hurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sorted that out in my head. It’s all just a blur most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: My weight? I… I haven’t really been keeping track of that. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I don’t get on it much anymore. I used to check it before church sometimes, because one dress would fit differently than another—funny the things you notice when you’re trying to look respectable.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve noticed. Maybe my clothes feel the same, maybe they don’t. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to feel any interest or excitement about things that are happening in the future?\n\nPatient: The future… I don’t really feel much when people talk about it. My daughter will sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know—of course I know—I love her. She’s my girl. But it’s like there’s no warmth getting through inside me.\n\nIt reminds me of when the radiators used to clank in the old house but the room still stayed cold. You could hear it working, but you didn’t feel it. That’s how it is. I can know something should matter, but I can’t feel the interest or excitement of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’ve paid attention to that. Moving slowly, restless—those sound like things someone else might notice better than I would.\n\nI do sit down and then get up sometimes, but then I’ll remember I meant to check whether the back door was locked, or I’ll see a cup on the table and think I should wash it, and then I don’t. My mother used to say I had “busy hands,” even when I was a girl, always folding napkins crooked at church suppers.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s different now. I can’t really say I’ve noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional numbness, like there's a wall inside you?\n\nPatient: Since… since it happened. Six days now, I think. It feels longer, but it’s been six days since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nI keep seeing that place in my mind, even though I try not to. They used to have bingo flyers on the board there, and a little coffee pot that never worked right… but this numbness, this wall you’re asking about—it started then. It hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the scale. I used to weigh myself on Sunday mornings, before church, because my sister always said a person should know where they stand, which is funny because she never knew where she put her glasses.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t think I’ve checked. My clothes feel like clothes, I guess. I can’t say I’ve lost a significant amount, no—not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts or memories of your grandson?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in when I’m not even trying to think about him.\n\nI’ll be folding a quilt square, or just reaching for my tea, and then all of a sudden I hear those shots again. I see him there, outside the community center, like it’s right in front of me all over again.\n\nI used to take him little muffins sometimes when they had things going on there… blueberry, usually, though he said he didn’t care for blueberries and still ate two. But now that’s not what comes first. It’s that picture. It just pushes in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I can’t say I’ve lost a significant amount, no—not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts or memories of your grandson?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in when I’m not even trying to think about him.\n\nI’ll be folding a quilt square, or just reaching for my tea, and then all of a sudden I hear those shots again. I see him there, outside the community center, like it’s right in front of me all over again.\n\nI used to take him little muffins sometimes when they had things going on there… blueberry, usually, though he said he didn’t care for blueberries and still ate two. But now that’s not what comes first. It’s that picture. It just pushes in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose I have. I stopped going to my walking club. We used to meet by the park entrance, and Margaret would always bring those little peppermints in her pocketbook—she’d offer them like they were medicine or something.

And the repast… the funeral meal, you know. Normally I’d be the one making lists, calling people, making sure there’s enough coffee and chicken and chairs. My family depends on me for that sort of thing. But I just… I can’t bring myself to help plan it. I look at the paper and my hand won’t move.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because since my grandson was killed, something in me just won’t come back. My daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand—she’s always had such warm hands, even as a little girl, I remember that—and I know I love her. I know that in my head. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like there’s a wall there, and I don’t know what to do with that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Six days… it’s been six days since he was killed, outside the community center. I keep thinking about that place, you know, they used to have little holiday things there, and I’d bring cookies sometimes… but yes, this feeling, it started then and it hasn’t let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any sadness or depressed mood since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if sadness is the right word. People keep saying “you must be so sad,” and I suppose that’s what it ought to be, but it feels harder to name than that. I sit there and think about him, and then my mind goes off to the soup I left on the stove or the sweater he wore once—blue, I think, though maybe it was green—and then I’m just… lost in it. So yes, maybe, but I can’t quite tell what I’m feeling most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to enjoy things or find pleasure in activities that you normally would?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, my daughter sits right there with me, holding my hand, and I know I love her—of course I do. She’s my girl. Her hands have always been warm, even when she was little, she’d come in from playing and grab onto me… but now I just can’t feel that warmth inside. It’s like I know what should be there, but there’s nothing I can reach.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson passed away that you have tried to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, I suppose… I keep sitting with my daughter, because that’s something that used to make me feel better, just being near her. She’ll hold my hand, and I know it’s her, I know I love her—she used to hold my hand crossing the street when she was little, funny how that turns around—but now I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. It’s like the feeling won’t come up, no matter how close she is.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or enjoyment at all since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I keep waiting for it, you know, like when my daughter sits right next to me and takes my hand. I know I love her, I know that as sure as anything—she’s always had that way of holding on, even when she was small—but since my grandson was killed, I just… I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like the feeling is supposed to be there, but it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. Since he was killed, it’s like the things I cared about are still there, but I’m not in them anymore. Even my daughter—she’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her, I know that… I remember when she was little, she used to tuck her fingers into mine so tight—but now I can’t feel that warmth inside me. It’s just… gone, or I can’t reach it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I keep turning things over, but I don’t know if guilt is the right word. When something like this happens, your mind goes looking in every cupboard for a reason, doesn’t it? I was doing that yesterday and found an old tin of buttons my mother kept—she never threw anything away, not even coat buttons. I suppose I’m the same way with thoughts. I keep them all and sort through them, but I can’t say I know what I’m guilty of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I sit there sometimes and everything feels so quiet, too quiet, like after the house has emptied out after Christmas dinner and all the plates are still stacked by the sink. My grandson used to—well, he’d always steal the corner piece of cake, you know, the one with the most icing.\n\nBut thoughts of hurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sorted that out in my head. It’s all just a blur most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: My weight? I… I haven’t really been keeping track of that. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I don’t get on it much anymore. I used to check it before church sometimes, because one dress would fit differently than another—funny the things you notice when you’re trying to look respectable.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve noticed. Maybe my clothes feel the same, maybe they don’t. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to feel any interest or excitement about things that are happening in the future?\n\nPatient: The future… I don’t really feel much when people talk about it. My daughter will sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know—of course I know—I love her. She’s my girl. But it’s like there’s no warmth getting through inside me.\n\nIt reminds me of when the radiators used to clank in the old house but the room still stayed cold. You could hear it working, but you didn’t feel it. That’s how it is. I can know something should matter, but I can’t feel the interest or excitement of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’ve paid attention to that. Moving slowly, restless—those sound like things someone else might notice better than I would.\n\nI do sit down and then get up sometimes, but then I’ll remember I meant to check whether the back door was locked, or I’ll see a cup on the table and think I should wash it, and then I don’t. My mother used to say I had “busy hands,” even when I was a girl, always folding napkins crooked at church suppers.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s different now. I can’t really say I’ve noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional numbness, like there's a wall inside you?\n\nPatient: Since… since it happened. Six days now, I think. It feels longer, but it’s been six days since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nI keep seeing that place in my mind, even though I try not to. They used to have bingo flyers on the board there, and a little coffee pot that never worked right… but this numbness, this wall you’re asking about—it started then. It hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the scale. I used to weigh myself on Sunday mornings, before church, because my sister always said a person should know where they stand, which is funny because she never knew where she put her glasses.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t think I’ve checked. My clothes feel like clothes, I guess. I can’t say I’ve lost a significant amount, no—not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts or memories of your grandson?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in when I’m not even trying to think about him.\n\nI’ll be folding a quilt square, or just reaching for my tea, and then all of a sudden I hear those shots again. I see him there, outside the community center, like it’s right in front of me all over again.\n\nI used to take him little muffins sometimes when they had things going on there… blueberry, usually, though he said he didn’t care for blueberries and still ate two. But now that’s not what comes first. It’s that picture. It just pushes in.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I stopped going to my walking club. We used to meet by the park entrance, and Margaret would always bring those little peppermints in her pocketbook—she’d offer them like they were medicine or something.\n\nAnd the repast… the funeral meal, you know. Normally I’d be the one making lists, calling people, making sure there’s enough coffee and chicken and chairs. My family depends on me for that sort of thing. But I just… I can’t bring myself to help plan it. I look at the paper and my hand won’t move.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

My mother used to say I had “busy hands,” even when I was a girl, always folding napkins crooked at church suppers.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s different now. I can’t really say I’ve noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional numbness, like there's a wall inside you?\n\nPatient: Since… since it happened. Six days now, I think. It feels longer, but it’s been six days since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nI keep seeing that place in my mind, even though I try not to. They used to have bingo flyers on the board there, and a little coffee pot that never worked right… but this numbness, this wall you’re asking about—it started then. It hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the scale. I used to weigh myself on Sunday mornings, before church, because my sister always said a person should know where they stand, which is funny because she never knew where she put her glasses.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t think I’ve checked. My clothes feel like clothes, I guess. I can’t say I’ve lost a significant amount, no—not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts or memories of your grandson?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in when I’m not even trying to think about him.\n\nI’ll be folding a quilt square, or just reaching for my tea, and then all of a sudden I hear those shots again. I see him there, outside the community center, like it’s right in front of me all over again.\n\nI used to take him little muffins sometimes when they had things going on there… blueberry, usually, though he said he didn’t care for blueberries and still ate two. But now that’s not what comes first. It’s that picture. It just pushes in.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I stopped going to my walking club. We used to meet by the park entrance, and Margaret would always bring those little peppermints in her pocketbook—she’d offer them like they were medicine or something.\n\nAnd the repast… the funeral meal, you know. Normally I’d be the one making lists, calling people, making sure there’s enough coffee and chicken and chairs. My family depends on me for that sort of thing. But I just… I can’t bring myself to help plan it. I look at the paper and my hand won’t move.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Yes… I suppose it’s not just the doing of things, it’s that even when something should feel good, it doesn’t quite reach me.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because since my grandson was killed, something in me just won’t come back. My daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand—she’s always had such warm hands, even as a little girl, I remember that—and I know I love her. I know that in my head. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like there’s a wall there, and I don’t know what to do with that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Six days… it’s been six days since he was killed, outside the community center. I keep thinking about that place, you know, they used to have little holiday things there, and I’d bring cookies sometimes… but yes, this feeling, it started then and it hasn’t let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any sadness or depressed mood since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if sadness is the right word. People keep saying “you must be so sad,” and I suppose that’s what it ought to be, but it feels harder to name than that. I sit there and think about him, and then my mind goes off to the soup I left on the stove or the sweater he wore once—blue, I think, though maybe it was green—and then I’m just… lost in it. So yes, maybe, but I can’t quite tell what I’m feeling most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to enjoy things or find pleasure in activities that you normally would?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, my daughter sits right there with me, holding my hand, and I know I love her—of course I do. She’s my girl. Her hands have always been warm, even when she was little, she’d come in from playing and grab onto me… but now I just can’t feel that warmth inside. It’s like I know what should be there, but there’s nothing I can reach.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson passed away that you have tried to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, I suppose… I keep sitting with my daughter, because that’s something that used to make me feel better, just being near her. She’ll hold my hand, and I know it’s her, I know I love her—she used to hold my hand crossing the street when she was little, funny how that turns around—but now I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. It’s like the feeling won’t come up, no matter how close she is.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or enjoyment at all since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I keep waiting for it, you know, like when my daughter sits right next to me and takes my hand. I know I love her, I know that as sure as anything—she’s always had that way of holding on, even when she was small—but since my grandson was killed, I just… I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like the feeling is supposed to be there, but it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. Since he was killed, it’s like the things I cared about are still there, but I’m not in them anymore. Even my daughter—she’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her, I know that… I remember when she was little, she used to tuck her fingers into mine so tight—but now I can’t feel that warmth inside me. It’s just… gone, or I can’t reach it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I keep turning things over, but I don’t know if guilt is the right word. When something like this happens, your mind goes looking in every cupboard for a reason, doesn’t it? I was doing that yesterday and found an old tin of buttons my mother kept—she never threw anything away, not even coat buttons. I suppose I’m the same way with thoughts. I keep them all and sort through them, but I can’t say I know what I’m guilty of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I sit there sometimes and everything feels so quiet, too quiet, like after the house has emptied out after Christmas dinner and all the plates are still stacked by the sink. My grandson used to—well, he’d always steal the corner piece of cake, you know, the one with the most icing.\n\nBut thoughts of hurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sorted that out in my head. It’s all just a blur most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: My weight? I… I haven’t really been keeping track of that. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I don’t get on it much anymore. I used to check it before church sometimes, because one dress would fit differently than another—funny the things you notice when you’re trying to look respectable.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve noticed. Maybe my clothes feel the same, maybe they don’t. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to feel any interest or excitement about things that are happening in the future?\n\nPatient: The future… I don’t really feel much when people talk about it. My daughter will sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know—of course I know—I love her. She’s my girl. But it’s like there’s no warmth getting through inside me.\n\nIt reminds me of when the radiators used to clank in the old house but the room still stayed cold. You could hear it working, but you didn’t feel it. That’s how it is. I can know something should matter, but I can’t feel the interest or excitement of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’ve paid attention to that. Moving slowly, restless—those sound like things someone else might notice better than I would.\n\nI do sit down and then get up sometimes, but then I’ll remember I meant to check whether the back door was locked, or I’ll see a cup on the table and think I should wash it, and then I don’t. My mother used to say I had “busy hands,” even when I was a girl, always folding napkins crooked at church suppers.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s different now. I can’t really say I’ve noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional numbness, like there's a wall inside you?\n\nPatient: Since… since it happened. Six days now, I think. It feels longer, but it’s been six days since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nI keep seeing that place in my mind, even though I try not to. My clothes feel like clothes, I guess. I can’t say I’ve lost a significant amount, no—not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts or memories of your grandson?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. But now that’s not what comes first. It’s that picture. It just pushes in.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I stopped going to my walking club. I look at the paper and my hand won’t move.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself having less interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it’s not just the doing of things, it’s that even when something should feel good, it doesn’t quite reach me.\n\nMy daughter sat right beside me the other day, holding my hand, and I know I love that child—of course I do. I knew it was love, but I couldn’t feel it the way I should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Even my daughter—she’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her, I know that… I remember when she was little, she used to tuck her fingers into mine so tight—but now I can’t feel that warmth inside me. It’s just… gone, or I can’t reach it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I keep turning things over, but I don’t know if guilt is the right word. When something like this happens, your mind goes looking in every cupboard for a reason, doesn’t it? I was doing that yesterday and found an old tin of buttons my mother kept—she never threw anything away, not even coat buttons. I suppose I’m the same way with thoughts. I keep them all and sort through them, but I can’t say I know what I’m guilty of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I sit there sometimes and everything feels so quiet, too quiet, like after the house has emptied out after Christmas dinner and all the plates are still stacked by the sink. My grandson used to—well, he’d always steal the corner piece of cake, you know, the one with the most icing.\n\nBut thoughts of hurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sorted that out in my head. It’s all just a blur most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: My weight? I… I haven’t really been keeping track of that. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I don’t get on it much anymore. I used to check it before church sometimes, because one dress would fit differently than another—funny the things you notice when you’re trying to look respectable.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve noticed. Maybe my clothes feel the same, maybe they don’t. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to feel any interest or excitement about things that are happening in the future?\n\nPatient: The future… I don’t really feel much when people talk about it. My daughter will sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know—of course I know—I love her. She’s my girl. But it’s like there’s no warmth getting through inside me.\n\nIt reminds me of when the radiators used to clank in the old house but the room still stayed cold. You could hear it working, but you didn’t feel it. That’s how it is. I can know something should matter, but I can’t feel the interest or excitement of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’ve paid attention to that. Moving slowly, restless—those sound like things someone else might notice better than I would.\n\nI do sit down and then get up sometimes, but then I’ll remember I meant to check whether the back door was locked, or I’ll see a cup on the table and think I should wash it, and then I don’t. My mother used to say I had “busy hands,” even when I was a girl, always folding napkins crooked at church suppers.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s different now. I can’t really say I’ve noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional numbness, like there's a wall inside you?\n\nPatient: Since… since it happened. Six days now, I think. It feels longer, but it’s been six days since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nI keep seeing that place in my mind, even though I try not to. They used to have bingo flyers on the board there, and a little coffee pot that never worked right… but this numbness, this wall you’re asking about—it started then. It hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the scale. I used to weigh myself on Sunday mornings, before church, because my sister always said a person should know where they stand, which is funny because she never knew where she put her glasses.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t think I’ve checked. My clothes feel like clothes, I guess. I can’t say I’ve lost a significant amount, no—not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts or memories of your grandson?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in when I’m not even trying to think about him.\n\nI’ll be folding a quilt square, or just reaching for my tea, and then all of a sudden I hear those shots again. I see him there, outside the community center, like it’s right in front of me all over again.\n\nI used to take him little muffins sometimes when they had things going on there… blueberry, usually, though he said he didn’t care for blueberries and still ate two. But now that’s not what comes first. It’s that picture. It just pushes in.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I stopped going to my walking club. We used to meet by the park entrance, and Margaret would always bring those little peppermints in her pocketbook—she’d offer them like they were medicine or something.\n\nAnd the repast… the funeral meal, you know. Normally I’d be the one making lists, calling people, making sure there’s enough coffee and chicken and chairs. My family depends on me for that sort of thing. But I just… I can’t bring myself to help plan it. I look at the paper and my hand won’t move.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself having less interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it’s not just the doing of things, it’s that even when something should feel good, it doesn’t quite reach me.\n\nMy daughter sat right beside me the other day, holding my hand, and I know I love that child—of course I do. I remember when she was little she used to put ribbons on the cat, poor thing just sat there like a saint—but now, with her hand in mine, I couldn’t feel the warmth of it inside me. I knew it was love, but I couldn’t feel it the way I should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yes… the walking club, I haven’t gone back to that at all. We used to meet regular, and I liked seeing the ladies—one of them always had a story about her neighbor’s dog getting loose, same dog every time, you’d think they’d fix that gate.

But no, I haven’t tried to go. I can’t seem to make myself.

And the repast planning… that’s the other thing. Usually I’d be right in the middle of it, making calls, counting chairs, seeing who’s bringing what. My family sort of counts on me for those things. But now I just look at it and… I can’t do it. I haven’t even really tried.

It’s like the feeling won’t come up, no matter how close she is.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or enjoyment at all since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I keep waiting for it, you know, like when my daughter sits right next to me and takes my hand. I know I love her, I know that as sure as anything—she’s always had that way of holding on, even when she was small—but since my grandson was killed, I just… I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like the feeling is supposed to be there, but it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. Since he was killed, it’s like the things I cared about are still there, but I’m not in them anymore.
