I keep turning things over, but I don’t know if guilt is the right word. When something like this happens, your mind goes looking in every cupboard for a reason, doesn’t it? I was doing that yesterday and found an old tin of buttons my mother kept—she never threw anything away, not even coat buttons. I suppose I’m the same way with thoughts. I keep them all and sort through them, but I can’t say I know what I’m guilty of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I sit there sometimes and everything feels so quiet, too quiet, like after the house has emptied out after Christmas dinner and all the plates are still stacked by the sink. My grandson used to—well, he’d always steal the corner piece of cake, you know, the one with the most icing.\n\nBut thoughts of hurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sorted that out in my head. It’s all just a blur most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: My weight? I… I haven’t really been keeping track of that. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I don’t get on it much anymore. I used to check it before church sometimes, because one dress would fit differently than another—funny the things you notice when you’re trying to look respectable.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve noticed. Maybe my clothes feel the same, maybe they don’t. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to feel any interest or excitement about things that are happening in the future?\n\nPatient: The future… I don’t really feel much when people talk about it. My daughter will sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know—of course I know—I love her. She’s my girl. But it’s like there’s no warmth getting through inside me.\n\nIt reminds me of when the radiators used to clank in the old house but the room still stayed cold. You could hear it working, but you didn’t feel it. That’s how it is. I can know something should matter, but I can’t feel the interest or excitement of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’ve paid attention to that. Moving slowly, restless—those sound like things someone else might notice better than I would.\n\nI do sit down and then get up sometimes, but then I’ll remember I meant to check whether the back door was locked, or I’ll see a cup on the table and think I should wash it, and then I don’t. My mother used to say I had “busy hands,” even when I was a girl, always folding napkins crooked at church suppers.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s different now. I can’t really say I’ve noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional numbness, like there's a wall inside you?\n\nPatient: Since… since it happened. Six days now, I think. It feels longer, but it’s been six days since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nI keep seeing that place in my mind, even though I try not to. They used to have bingo flyers on the board there, and a little coffee pot that never worked right… but this numbness, this wall you’re asking about—it started then. It hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the scale. I used to weigh myself on Sunday mornings, before church, because my sister always said a person should know where they stand, which is funny because she never knew where she put her glasses.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t think I’ve checked. My clothes feel like clothes, I guess. I can’t say I’ve lost a significant amount, no—not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts or memories of your grandson?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in when I’m not even trying to think about him.\n\nI’ll be folding a quilt square, or just reaching for my tea, and then all of a sudden I hear those shots again. I see him there, outside the community center, like it’s right in front of me all over again.\n\nI used to take him little muffins sometimes when they had things going on there… blueberry, usually, though he said he didn’t care for blueberries and still ate two. But now that’s not what comes first. It’s that picture. It just pushes in.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I stopped going to my walking club. We used to meet by the park entrance, and Margaret would always bring those little peppermints in her pocketbook—she’d offer them like they were medicine or something.\n\nAnd the repast… the funeral meal, you know. Normally I’d be the one making lists, calling people, making sure there’s enough coffee and chicken and chairs. My family depends on me for that sort of thing. But I just… I can’t bring myself to help plan it. I look at the paper and my hand won’t move.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself having less interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it’s not just the doing of things, it’s that even when something should feel good, it doesn’t quite reach me.\n\nMy daughter sat right beside me the other day, holding my hand, and I know I love that child—of course I do. I remember when she was little she used to put ribbons on the cat, poor thing just sat there like a saint—but now, with her hand in mine, I couldn’t feel the warmth of it inside me. I knew it was love, but I couldn’t feel it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy doing before your grandson passed away that you have not tried to do since then?\n\nPatient: Yes… the walking club, I haven’t gone back to that at all. We used to meet regular, and I liked seeing the ladies—one of them always had a story about her neighbor’s dog getting loose, same dog every time, you’d think they’d fix that gate.\n\nBut no, I haven’t tried to go. I can’t seem to make myself.\n\nAnd the repast planning… that’s the other thing. Usually I’d be right in the middle of it, making calls, counting chairs, seeing who’s bringing what. But it’s like there’s no warmth getting through inside me.\n\nIt reminds me of when the radiators used to clank in the old house but the room still stayed cold. You could hear it working, but you didn’t feel it. That’s how it is. I can know something should matter, but I can’t feel the interest or excitement of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’ve paid attention to that. Moving slowly, restless—those sound like things someone else might notice better than I would.\n\nI do sit down and then get up sometimes, but then I’ll remember I meant to check whether the back door was locked, or I’ll see a cup on the table and think I should wash it, and then I don’t. My mother used to say I had “busy hands,” even when I was a girl, always folding napkins crooked at church suppers.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s different now. I can’t really say I’ve noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional numbness, like there's a wall inside you?\n\nPatient: Since… since it happened. Six days now, I think. It feels longer, but it’s been six days since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nI keep seeing that place in my mind, even though I try not to. They used to have bingo flyers on the board there, and a little coffee pot that never worked right… but this numbness, this wall you’re asking about—it started then. It hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the scale. I used to weigh myself on Sunday mornings, before church, because my sister always said a person should know where they stand, which is funny because she never knew where she put her glasses.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t think I’ve checked. My clothes feel like clothes, I guess. I can’t say I’ve lost a significant amount, no—not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts or memories of your grandson?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in when I’m not even trying to think about him.\n\nI’ll be folding a quilt square, or just reaching for my tea, and then all of a sudden I hear those shots again. I see him there, outside the community center, like it’s right in front of me all over again.\n\nI used to take him little muffins sometimes when they had things going on there… blueberry, usually, though he said he didn’t care for blueberries and still ate two. But now that’s not what comes first. It’s that picture. It just pushes in.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I stopped going to my walking club. We used to meet by the park entrance, and Margaret would always bring those little peppermints in her pocketbook—she’d offer them like they were medicine or something.\n\nAnd the repast… the funeral meal, you know. Normally I’d be the one making lists, calling people, making sure there’s enough coffee and chicken and chairs. My family depends on me for that sort of thing. But I just… I can’t bring myself to help plan it. I look at the paper and my hand won’t move.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself having less interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it’s not just the doing of things, it’s that even when something should feel good, it doesn’t quite reach me.\n\nMy daughter sat right beside me the other day, holding my hand, and I know I love that child—of course I do. I remember when she was little she used to put ribbons on the cat, poor thing just sat there like a saint—but now, with her hand in mine, I couldn’t feel the warmth of it inside me. I knew it was love, but I couldn’t feel it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy doing before your grandson passed away that you have not tried to do since then?\n\nPatient: Yes… the walking club, I haven’t gone back to that at all. We used to meet regular, and I liked seeing the ladies—one of them always had a story about her neighbor’s dog getting loose, same dog every time, you’d think they’d fix that gate.\n\nBut no, I haven’t tried to go. I can’t seem to make myself.\n\nAnd the repast planning… that’s the other thing. Usually I’d be right in the middle of it, making calls, counting chairs, seeing who’s bringing what. My family sort of counts on me for those things. But now I just look at it and… I can’t do it. I haven’t even really tried.\n\nDoctor: How many days have you been feeling this way, without any improvement at all?\n\nPatient: Six days… it’s been six days now. Since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nI keep thinking about that place, you know, how they used to have those bulletin boards with all the little flyers—bingo night, coats for the winter drive, somebody’s lost cat. Six days, and it hasn’t let up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because since my grandson was killed, something in me just won’t come back. My daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand—she’s always had such warm hands, even as a little girl, I remember that—and I know I love her. I know that in my head. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like there’s a wall there, and I don’t know what to do with that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Six days… it’s been six days since he was killed, outside the community center. I keep thinking about that place, you know, they used to have little holiday things there, and I’d bring cookies sometimes… but yes, this feeling, it started then and it hasn’t let up since.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any sadness or depressed mood since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if sadness is the right word. People keep saying “you must be so sad,” and I suppose that’s what it ought to be, but it feels harder to name than that. I sit there and think about him, and then my mind goes off to the soup I left on the stove or the sweater he wore once—blue, I think, though maybe it was green—and then I’m just… lost in it. So yes, maybe, but I can’t quite tell what I’m feeling most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to enjoy things or find pleasure in activities that you normally would?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, my daughter sits right there with me, holding my hand, and I know I love her—of course I do. She’s my girl. Her hands have always been warm, even when she was little, she’d come in from playing and grab onto me… but now I just can’t feel that warmth inside. It’s like I know what should be there, but there’s nothing I can reach.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson passed away that you have tried to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, I suppose… I keep sitting with my daughter, because that’s something that used to make me feel better, just being near her. She’ll hold my hand, and I know it’s her, I know I love her—she used to hold my hand crossing the street when she was little, funny how that turns around—but now I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. It’s like the feeling won’t come up, no matter how close she is.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or enjoyment at all since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I keep waiting for it, you know, like when my daughter sits right next to me and takes my hand. I know I love her, I know that as sure as anything—she’s always had that way of holding on, even when she was small—but since my grandson was killed, I just… I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like the feeling is supposed to be there, but it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. Since he was killed, it’s like the things I cared about are still there, but I’m not in them anymore. Even my daughter—she’ll sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know I love her, I know that… I remember when she was little, she used to tuck her fingers into mine so tight—but now I can’t feel that warmth inside me. It’s just… gone, or I can’t reach it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I keep turning things over, but I don’t know if guilt is the right word. When something like this happens, your mind goes looking in every cupboard for a reason, doesn’t it? I was doing that yesterday and found an old tin of buttons my mother kept—she never threw anything away, not even coat buttons. I suppose I’m the same way with thoughts. I keep them all and sort through them, but I can’t say I know what I’m guilty of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I sit there sometimes and everything feels so quiet, too quiet, like after the house has emptied out after Christmas dinner and all the plates are still stacked by the sink. My grandson used to—well, he’d always steal the corner piece of cake, you know, the one with the most icing.\n\nBut thoughts of hurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sorted that out in my head. It’s all just a blur most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: My weight? I… I haven’t really been keeping track of that. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I don’t get on it much anymore. I used to check it before church sometimes, because one dress would fit differently than another—funny the things you notice when you’re trying to look respectable.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve noticed. Maybe my clothes feel the same, maybe they don’t. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to feel any interest or excitement about things that are happening in the future?\n\nPatient: The future… I don’t really feel much when people talk about it. My daughter will sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know—of course I know—I love her. She’s my girl. But it’s like there’s no warmth getting through inside me.\n\nIt reminds me of when the radiators used to clank in the old house but the room still stayed cold. You could hear it working, but you didn’t feel it. That’s how it is. I can know something should matter, but I can’t feel the interest or excitement of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’ve paid attention to that. Moving slowly, restless—those sound like things someone else might notice better than I would.\n\nI do sit down and then get up sometimes, but then I’ll remember I meant to check whether the back door was locked, or I’ll see a cup on the table and think I should wash it, and then I don’t. My mother used to say I had “busy hands,” even when I was a girl, always folding napkins crooked at church suppers.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s different now. I can’t really say I’ve noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional numbness, like there's a wall inside you?\n\nPatient: Since… since it happened. Six days now, I think. It feels longer, but it’s been six days since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nI keep seeing that place in my mind, even though I try not to. They used to have bingo flyers on the board there, and a little coffee pot that never worked right… but this numbness, this wall you’re asking about—it started then. It hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the scale. I used to weigh myself on Sunday mornings, before church, because my sister always said a person should know where they stand, which is funny because she never knew where she put her glasses.\n\nBut since all this… I don’t think I’ve checked. My clothes feel like clothes, I guess. I can’t say I’ve lost a significant amount, no—not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts or memories of your grandson?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in when I’m not even trying to think about him.\n\nI’ll be folding a quilt square, or just reaching for my tea, and then all of a sudden I hear those shots again. I see him there, outside the community center, like it’s right in front of me all over again.\n\nI used to take him little muffins sometimes when they had things going on there… blueberry, usually, though he said he didn’t care for blueberries and still ate two. But now that’s not what comes first. It’s that picture. It just pushes in.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I stopped going to my walking club. We used to meet by the park entrance, and Margaret would always bring those little peppermints in her pocketbook—she’d offer them like they were medicine or something.\n\nAnd the repast… the funeral meal, you know. Normally I’d be the one making lists, calling people, making sure there’s enough coffee and chicken and chairs. My family depends on me for that sort of thing. But I just… I can’t bring myself to help plan it. I look at the paper and my hand won’t move.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself having less interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it’s not just the doing of things, it’s that even when something should feel good, it doesn’t quite reach me.\n\nMy daughter sat right beside me the other day, holding my hand, and I know I love that child—of course I do. I remember when she was little she used to put ribbons on the cat, poor thing just sat there like a saint—but now, with her hand in mine, I couldn’t feel the warmth of it inside me. I knew it was love, but I couldn’t feel it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy doing before your grandson passed away that you have not tried to do since then?\n\nPatient: Yes… the walking club, I haven’t gone back to that at all. We used to meet regular, and I liked seeing the ladies—one of them always had a story about her neighbor’s dog getting loose, same dog every time, you’d think they’d fix that gate.\n\nBut no, I haven’t tried to go. I can’t seem to make myself.\n\nAnd the repast planning… that’s the other thing. Usually I’d be right in the middle of it, making calls, counting chairs, seeing who’s bringing what. My family sort of counts on me for those things. But now I just look at it and… I can’t do it. I haven’t even really tried.\n\nDoctor: How many days have you been feeling this way, without any improvement at all?\n\nPatient: Six days… it’s been six days now. Since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\nI keep thinking about that place, you know, how they used to have those bulletin boards with all the little flyers—bingo night, coats for the winter drive, somebody’s lost cat. Six days, and it hasn’t let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a lot of weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know. I haven’t been weighing myself.\n\nMy clothes—well, they feel about the same, I think. I haven’t noticed anything like that. Though I did stand by the kitchen scale the other morning, not the kind you weigh yourself on, the old one for flour, and I thought about how my mother used to measure everything by hand anyway… never trusted a scale.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve lost a lot of weight. I just haven’t checked.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
